\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\tilde{f}*\tilde{g}\right](\bm{x})=(2\pi)^{n}\mathcal{F}\left[f\right](\bm{k})\mathcal{F}\left[g\right](\bm{k}).

(A.13)

Or slightly more compactly:

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{n}\mathcal{F}\left[fg\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}*\tilde{g},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left[f*g\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}\tilde{g},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\tilde{f}\tilde{g}\right]

\displaystyle=f*g,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\tilde{f}*\tilde{g}\right]

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{n}fg.

A.1.1
Power spectra

We use the definition

\expectationvalue{\tilde{\delta}(\kk)\tilde{\delta}(\kk')}=(2\pi)^{n}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}+\bm{k}^{\prime})P(k)\,,

(A.14)

for the power spectrum, to keep the factors of
2\pi
with the momentum space Dirac delta in keeping with the Fourier conventions above. However, many places define simply

\expectationvalue{\tilde{\delta}(\kk)\tilde{\delta}(\kk')}=\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}+\bm{k}^{\prime})P(k).

(A.15)

such that the power spectrum measures the root mean square Fourier amplitude of the field for real fields.

The conventions for both the power spectrum and Fourier transforms in
Bernardeau et al. (2002)
swapping the role of the
2\pi
s. This affects how the variance is defined for example. The (unsmoothed) linear variance is by our conventions

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm L}^{2}=\expectationvalue{\delta_L^2(\xx)}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_1}\differential[3]{\kk_2}}{(2\pi)^{3}(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})\cdot\bm{x}}\underbrace{\expectationvalue{\delta(\kk_1)\delta(\kk_2)}}_{(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})P_{\rm L}(k_{1})}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{x}}P_{L}(k_{1})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential{k}}{2\pi^{2}}k^{2}P_{\rm L}(k)

(A.16)

where the last line comes out of doing the angular integral to get
4\pi k^{2}
and now we integrate just on the magnitude of
k
. With the role of the
2\pi
s swapped in both places, the linear variance is instead

\sigma_{\rm L}^{2}=\int\differential{k}4\pi k^{2}P_{\rm L}(k).

(A.17)

Whether
(2\pi)^{n}
in the definition of
\expectationvalue{\delta(k)\delta(k')}
should match the power of the
(2\pi)^{n}
in the Fourier conventions determines whether the correlation function in real space is the Fourier transform of the power spectrum or not. For example, if we keep my Fourier conventions but define the power spectrum
\expectationvalue{\delta(k)\delta(k')}=P(k)\delta_{\rm D}(k+k^{\prime})
then we have

\xi(r)=\expectationvalue{\delta(\xx)\delta(\xx+\bm{r})}=\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{x}}e^{i\bm{k}_{2}\cdot(\bm{x}+\bm{r})}\underbrace{\expectationvalue{\tilde{\delta}(\kk_1)\tilde{\delta}(\kk_2)}}_{P(k_{2})\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})}\,,

(A.18)

which, when integrating out
\bm{k}_{1}
gives

\xi(r)=\expectationvalue{\delta(\xx)\delta(\xx+\bm{r})}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\bm{k}_{2}\cdot\bm{r}}P(k_{2})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\mathcal{F}[P(\bm{k}_{2})](\bm{r})\,,

(A.19)

where, if we’d used consistent Fourier conventions and power spectrum definitions, this would have just been the Fourier transform, which is nicer.

A.1.2
Window functions

For obtaining smoothed fields, different window functions are commonly used. In this thesis smoothing is exclusively done with real space top-hat filters unless otherwise specified, as these filters are well suited to LDT and certain analytic properties for SPT.

Smoothing in real space is done with a convolution

\delta_{R}(\bm{x})=\int\differential[n]{\bm{x}}^{\prime}W_{R}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\delta(\bm{x}^{\prime})=(W_{R}*\delta)(\bm{x})\,,

(A.20)

which in Fourier space is simply a product.

\delta_{R}(\bm{k})=\tilde{W}_{R}(\bm{k})\delta(\bm{k}).

(A.21)

Gaussian filters
are Gaussian in both real and Fourier space

\displaystyle W_{R}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi R^{2})^{n/2}}\exp(-\frac{x^2}{2R^2})

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{R}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=\exp(-\frac{1}{2}R^2 k^2).

(A.22)

These filters are convenient analytically as they are nicely differentiable in both real and Fourier space, and some analytic properties can be derived for Gaussianly smoothed fields (such as the statistics of critical points as discussed in Chapter
8
). In numerical applications however their infinite extent in both real and Fourier space can work against them, introducing the need for truncation and difficulty navigating masked data.

Spherical top-hat
filters in real space (3D)

\displaystyle W_{R}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4\pi R^{3}}\Theta\left(\absolutevalue{\xx}-R\right)

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{R}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4\pi R^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\differential[3]{\xx}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}\Theta(\absolutevalue{x}-R)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4\pi R^{3}}\int_{\phi=0}^{2\pi}\int_{\cos\theta=-1}^{1}\int_{r=0}^{R}r^{2}e^{-ikr\cos\theta}\differential{r}\mathrm{d}\cos\theta\differential{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2R^{3}}\int_{0}^{R}\differential{r}r^{2}\frac{2\sin(kr)}{kr}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{(kR)^{3}}\left(\sin(kR)-kR\cos(kR)\right)=3\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{J_{3/2}(kR)}{(kR)^{3/2}}

while in 2D (relevant for gravitational lensing analysis for example) we have

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{R}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi R^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{R}re^{-ikr\cos\phi}\differential{r}\differential{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi R^{2}}\int_{0}^{R}r(2\pi J_{0}(kr))\differential{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{2J_{1}(kR)}{kR}

where
J_{n}(z)
is the
n^{\rm th}
order Bessel function of the first kind. Appendix C of
Bernardeau et al. (2002)
has various mathematical properties of
N
-dimensional spherical top-hat filters.

It is sometimes useful to match features between fields smoothed with different filters. The appropriate prescription will vary depending on the case of interest, but we note here that

•

Matching the FWHM of the a Gaussian and real-space top-hat filter in Fourier space requires

R_{\rm TH}\simeq\frac{2.49826}{\sqrt{2\ln 2}}R_{\rm G}\approx 2.121R_{\rm G}.

(A.23)

•

Matching the mass enclosed by the filters (in 3D space) produces a ratio of

R_{\rm TH}\simeq 1.56R_{\rm G}.

(A.24)

A.2
Moments and cumulants of probability distributions

Here we review certain standard statistical quantities. For a continuous random variable
X
with an associated probability density function
\mathcal{P}_{X}
, the moments of
X
are expectation values of
X
relative to this probability density

M_{n}=\expectationvalue{X^n}=\int\mathcal{P}_{X}(X=x)x^{n}\differential{x}.

(A.25)

If the
\mathcal{P}
is for example a phase-space distribution
f
, then the moments with respect to the momentum
\bm{p}
are physical quantities such as the density and momentum flux.
1
1

1

Throughout this work we assume the ergodic hypothesis where we can replace ensemble averages with spatial averages over one realisation of a random field. This requires that spatial correlations decay sufficiently rapidly with separation that many statistically independent volumes are contained in one realisation.
The moment generating function is a formal power series in some auxiliary variable
\lambda
where the coefficients are these moments,

\mathcal{M}_{X}(\lambda)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\expectationvalue{X^n}}{n!}\lambda^{n}=1+\lambda\expectationvalue{X}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\expectationvalue{X^2}+\dots

(A.26)

which provides a useful calculation tool as from the moment generating function the
n^{\rm th}
moment can be obtained by taking the
n^{\rm th}
derivative and evaluating at
\lambda=0
. The moment generating function is related to the PDF by Laplace transform

\mathcal{M}_{X}(\lambda)=\expectationvalue{e^{\lambda X}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\lambda X}\mathcal{P}_{X}(x)\differential{x}.

(A.27)

Similar to the moments of a distribution are the cumulants of a distribution which are the “connected part” of the moments. This is understood in the following sense, part of the information content in a moment of a distribution simply comes products of the lower moments. The “connected part” is the independent part which is obtained by subtracting off a sum over partitions, where each partition then involves products of those corresponding moments. That is

\displaystyle\langle X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{n}\rangle_{c}=

\displaystyle\phantom{+}\langle X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\expectationvalue{X_{\sigma(1)}\dots X_{\sigma(\abs{\mathcal{S}})}}

(A.28)

where
\mathcal{S}
is any proper partition of
\{1,\dots,n\}
, and
\sigma
is a subset of that partition. I find this is best understood through examples. The second cumulant is simply the variance (the same as the second central moment)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}=\langle X^{2}\rangle-\langle X\rangle\langle X\rangle.

(A.29)

The third cumulant is

\displaystyle k_{3}=\langle X^{3}\rangle_{c}

\displaystyle=\langle X^{3}\rangle-3\langle X^{2}\rangle\langle X\rangle.

(A.30)

where the factor of three arises from the 3 different ways that
XXX
can be partitioned into a set of 2
X
s and 1
X
. This partitioning can be represented diagramatically, as we see in the discussion of correlation functions in Chapter
3
.

The cumulant generating function
2
2

2

One could instead simply define the cumulants as the coefficients which are generated by
\log\mathcal{M}(\lambda)
and then find the connected part interpretation.
(CGF),
\phi_{X}(\lambda)
is again a power series with the cumulants as coefficients, and is related to the moment generating function by exponentiation
3
3

3

For readers familiar with QFT and Feynman diagrams, the moment generating function is similar to the path integral functions partition functions
Z_{j}
which generate vacuum correlation values
\langle 0|\phi(\bm{x}_{1})\dots\phi(\bm{x}_{n})|0\rangle
by successive differentiation, while the cumulant generating function is usually written
W_{j}=\log Z_{j}
and generates only the
connected
Feynman diagrams. There is usually a slight difference as the partition function is usually defined as the Fourier transform rather than the Laplace transform, requiring different powers of
i
, and changing technical convergence effects, but the spirit is the same.

\displaystyle\phi_{X}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\ln\mathcal{M}_{X}(\lambda)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\expectationvalue{X^n}_{c}}{n!}\lambda^{n}=k_{1}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}k_{\lambda}^{2}+\dots

(A.31)

\displaystyle k_{n}

\displaystyle=\derivative[n]{\phi_X}{\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda=0}

(A.32)

The cumulants are simpler objects to work characterise a random variable. For example, if a random variable has a Gaussian PDF, its moments are all non-zero, but moments of degree 3 or higher are simply combinations of the mean and variance of the distribution (this is the basis for Wick’s theorem for Gaussian fields). In contrast, the only non-zero cumulants of a Gaussian are
k_{1},k_{2}
, with all higher cumulants vanishing.

Note that when the moment/cumulant generating function exists, the associated PDF is uniquely defined by it. However this does not mean that the moments/cumulants uniquely define the PDF, as it is possible for all the moment to exist but the limit which defines the generating function to not converge, as is the case for the log-normal distribution.

For joint distributions the joint cumulant generating function reads

\displaystyle\phi_{X,Y}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\log(\mathcal{M}_{X,Y}(\lambda_1,\lambda_2))=\log\expectationvalue{e^{\lambda_1 X + \lambda_2 Y}}

(A.33a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}\frac{\expectationvalue{X^pY^q}}{p!q!}\lambda_{1}^{p}\lambda_{2}^{q}-1.

(A.33b)

This then allows the CGF to be defined on any linear combination of random variables. The PDF can then be obtained from the CGF via inverse Laplace transform
(see
Balian & Schaeffer, 1989
;
Bernardeau, 2013
, for a detailed derivation of this)

P_{X}(x)=\int_{\alpha-i\infty}^{\alpha+i\infty}\frac{\differential{\lambda}}{2\pi i}\exp(-\lambda x + \phi_X(\lambda)).

(A.34)

Cumulants and moments can be expressed in terms of each other via the partial Bell polynomials
B_{n,k}

\displaystyle k_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!B_{n,k}(M_{1},\dots,M_{n-k+1})\,,

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}B_{n,k}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-k+1}).

A.2.1
Reduced cumulants and scaled CGF

Taking the cosmic density field as the random field of interest, the quantities

S_{n}=\frac{\expectationvalue{\delta^n}_{c}}{\expectationvalue{\delta^2}_{c}^{n-1}}=\frac{k_{n}}{k_{2}^{n-1}}

(A.36)

are called the reduced cumulants, where
k_{2}=\sigma^{2}
is the variance of the density. These are of importance in this work as they are independent of cosmology down to mildly non-linear scales
(
Peebles, 1980
;
Baugh et al., 1995
)
, and are at the root of Large Deviations Theory, which we apply to the matter PDF. The scaled cumulant generating function (SCGF) is defined with these reduced cumulants as the coefficients, in the limit of vanishing variance,

\varphi_{X}(\lambda)=\lim_{k_{2}\to 0}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S_{n}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}=\lim_{k_{2}\to 0}k_{2}\,\phi_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda}{k_{2}}\right).

(A.37)

A.3
Hypergeometric functions

For general information on special functions and properties of these functions, see
1, 1
.

The Gauss hypergeometric function is defined by a power series

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{k}(b)_{k}z^{k}}{(c)_{k}k!}=1+\frac{ab}{c}\frac{z}{1!}+\frac{a(a+1)b(b+1)}{c(c+1)}\frac{z^{2}}{2!}+\dots,

(A.38)

where
(n)_{k}
is the rising Pochhammer symbol (like a factorial but the terms raise by 1 instead of decreasing by 1)

(n)_{k}=\begin{cases}1&k=0\\
n(n+1)\dots(n+k-1)&k>0.\end{cases}

(A.39)

These Pochhammer symbols can also be represented via the Gamma function as

(n)_{k}=\frac{\Gamma(n+k)}{\Gamma(n)}.

(A.40)

This can be generalised the generalised hypergeometric series
{}_{p}F_{q}
which takes
p
arguments in the numerator and
q
arguments in the denominator of the series

{}_{p}F_{q}(a_{1},\dots,a_{p};b_{1},\dots,b_{q};z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a_{1})_{k}\dots(a_{p})_{k}}{(b_{1})_{k}\dots(b_{q})_{k}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}.

(A.41)

Such functions come up as solutions to differential equations of the form

z(z-1)\derivative[2]{w}{z}+[c-(a+b+1)z]\derivative{w}{z}-abw=0,

(A.42)

which has three regular singular points at
z=0,1,\infty
. Any second order linear differential equation with 3 singular points can be converted to this form by change of variables. Solutions to this differential equation are built out of this function
{}_{2}F_{1}
and polynomials in
z
times it.

Another specific hypergeometric function of use to is the hypergeometric function

{}_{0}F_{1}(a;z)=\sum_{k}\frac{z^{n}}{(a)_{k}k!}=\sum_{k}\frac{\Gamma(a)}{\Gamma(k+a)\Gamma(k+1)}z^{n}

(A.43)

which arises in the minimal tree model for the matter covariance in Chapter
6
. This solves the differential equation

\left[z\derivative[2]{z}+a\derivative{z}-1\right]F=0.

(A.44)

This hypergeometric function is closely related to the better known Bessel functions

J_{n}(x)=\frac{(\frac{1}{2}x)^{2}}{n!}{}_{0}F_{1}\left(n+1;-\frac{1}{4}x^{2}\right),

(A.45)

where
J_{n}(x)
is the Bessel function of the first kind. The regularised confluent hypergeometric function
{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(a;z)={}_{0}F_{1}(a;z)/\Gamma(a)
is related to the modified Bessel functions
I_{n}(x)
by

{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(a;z)=z^{\frac{1-a}{2}}I_{a-1}(2\sqrt{z}).

(A.46)

A.4
Asymptotic evaluation of integrals

If there is one non-linear problem which physicists are well equipped to handle, it is the simple harmonic oscillator and relatedly, the evaluation of Gaussian integrals. Here we leverage this power to evaluate integrals of the form

I(\nu)=\int\differential{t}f(t)\exp(\nu g(t)),

(A.47)

where
\nu
is some large parameter. Integrals of this form appear frequently in physics. In this work the relevant examples are

1.

The theorems of LDT rely on evaluation of integrals with rate function
\psi
and a large driving parameter
n
, which is given by the
1/\sigma^{2}
in the context of cosmological PDFs.

2.

The path integral formulation of quantum mechanics relies on integrals over
e^{iS/\hbar}
where
S
is a classical action. The semiclassical limit is then obtained in the
\hbar\to 0
limit.

3.

The wave field in optics can be expressed as the oscillatory Fresnel-Kirchhoff integral. The geometric optics limit is obtained as the wavenumber
\nu\to\infty
.

Such integrals can be evaluated in the
\nu\to\infty
limit as asymptotic series in powers of
\nu
through a variety of techniques. Here we only discuss the leading order term, which is known as the Saddle Point Approximation. Further details on these methods and their extensions can be found in standard texts on asymptotic analysis e.g.
Bender & Orszag (1978)
.

A.4.1
Laplace’s method

Laplace’s method applies to real integrals

I(\nu)=\int_{a}^{b}\differential{t}f(t)\exp(\nu g(t))\,,

(A.48)

where
f,g
are both real functions and the integration range is along the real line. The idea behind this method is that the main contribution to this integral as
\nu
becomes large is from points where
g(t)
is maximised, as the integrand exponentially drops off away from such points. For concreteness we take the location of this maximum
t=t_{*}
to not occur at the endpoint
a<t_{*}<b
, though similar arguments can be made in those cases. We then approximate the integral
I(\nu)
by restricting the range of integration to a small region around the stationary point

I(\nu)\sim\int_{t_{*}-\epsilon}^{t_{*}+\epsilon}\differential{t}f(t)e^{\nu g(t)}.

(A.49)

We then Taylor expand both
f
and
g
about this stationary point, then further Taylor expand the exponential to bring down any terms which are more than quadratic in
t
:

\displaystyle I(\nu)

\displaystyle\sim\int_{t_{*}-\epsilon}^{t_{*}+\epsilon}\differential{t}f(t)e^{\nu g(t)}

(A.50a)

\displaystyle\sim\int_{t_{*}-\epsilon}^{t_{*}-\epsilon}\differential{t}(f(t_{*})+\dots)e^{\nu\left(g(t_{*})+\frac{1}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(t_{*})(t-t_{*})^{2}+\dots\right)}.

(A.50b)

The final trick comes from realising that we can now push the limits of the integral back out to
\pm\infty
and at the cost of introducing only exponentially small errors,

I(\nu)\sim\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential{t}(f(t_{*})+\dots)e^{\nu\left(g(t_{*})+\frac{1}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(t_{*})(t-t_{*})^{2}+\dots\right)}\,,

(A.51)

which is now a Gaussian integral that can be evaluated exactly

I(\nu)\sim f(t_{*})e^{\nu g(t_{*})}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-\nu g^{\prime\prime}(t_{*})}}+\dots.

(A.52)

Higher order terms (with respect to
\nu
) in this series could be obtained by keeping further terms in the Taylor expansions of
f(t)
and
\exp(\nu g(t))
, then performing the resulting moments of Gaussian integrals exactly.

If there are multiple stationary points, and they are sufficiently separated compared to
\nu^{-1}
, then the total integral can be approximated by simply summing over these stationary points.

A.4.2
Method of stationary phase

The method of stationary phase is similar in spirit to Laplace’s method, and deals with integrals with oscillatory integrands

I(\nu)=\int_{a}^{b}\differential{t}f(t)e^{i\nu g(t)}.

(A.53)

The strategy for such integrals follows the same spirit as Laplace’s method. In this case, the integrand is not exponentially suppressed as we move away from stationary points of
g
, as the magnitude of the integrand is controlled by the size of
f
. However, since the integrand is so oscillatory, it will largely cancel itself out. Near points
t_{*}
where
g^{\prime}(t_{*})=0
, the frequency of oscillations decreases, leading to such points dominating the integral. The method to obtain this leading contribution then follows the same steps as in Laplace’s method:

1.

Restricting the integration range to be isolated around each stationary point (provided the stationary points are sufficiently separated compared to
\nu^{-1}
).

2.

Taylor expanding
f
and
g
about
t=t_{*}
, then further Taylor expanding the exponential term so that only quadratic or lower terms are left in the exponent.

3.

Replacing the limits of the integral by
\pm\infty
, and performing the resulting Gaussian integrals.

The contribution from a single stationary point in this case is

\displaystyle I_{t_{*}}(\nu)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\nu\absolutevalue{g''(t_*)}}}f(t_{*})\exp\left(i\nu g(t_{*})+\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(g^{\prime\prime}(t_{*}))\right),

(A.54)

which has the same structure as the result from Laplace’s method, but with an additional phase factor
\exp(\pm i \pi)
depending on the sign of the second derivative, called the Morse index.

We note for completeness two useful direct extensions to equation (
A.54
). Firstly, if the first
(p-1)
derivatives of
g
are all 0 at a stationary point, but
g^{(p)}(t_{*})\neq 0
, the contribution receives a modification
(
Bender & Orszag, 1978
)

\displaystyle I_{t_{*}}^{\rm SPA}(\nu)=f(t_{*})\left[\frac{p!}{\nu\absolutevalue{g^{(p)}(t_*)}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{p})}{p}\,\exp\left(i\nu g(t_{*})+\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(g^{(p)}(t_{*}))\right).

(A.55)

Secondly, we can extend this formalism to integrals over
n
-dimensional space. In this case

\displaystyle I_{t_{*}}^{\rm SPA}(\nu)=\frac{f(\bm{t}_{*})}{\absolutevalue{\det\mathsf{H}(\bm{t}_*)}^{1/2}}\exp\left(i\nu g(\bm{t}_{*})+\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(\mathsf{H}(\bm{t}_{*}))\right),

(A.56)

where
\mathsf{H}_{ij}=\partialderivative*{g}{t_i}{t_j}
is the Hessian matrix (provided
\mathsf{H}(\bm{t}_{*})\neq 0
). The signature of the Hessian is the difference between the number of negative and positive eigenvalues. Similar results hold for Laplace’s method.

As a note of caution, the stationary phase approximation is not well suited to obtaining terms higher than this leading contribution in
\nu
. It performs worse than its equivalent for non-oscillatory integrals as in Laplace’s method. This is because in Laplace’s method, the errors introduced by steps such as pushing the integration range to
\infty
introduce exponentially small corrections, while in stationary phase, the corrections are generally only algebraically small
(see Chapter 6 of
Bender & Orszag, 1978
)
. Thus higher order terms depend both on further terms in the Taylor expansion about the stationary points,
and
on contributions from non-stationary points. For this reason, obtaining next order (in
\nu
) corrections should be done either by asymptotic matching, or by considering the full steepest descent contour.

A.4.3
Method of steepest descent

The most general form of these integrals is

I(\nu)\sim\int_{\gamma}\differential{t}f(t)e^{\nu g(t)},

(A.57)

where now
f
and
g
are allowed to be complex and integration is performed over a curve in the complex
t
-plane. Evaluation of such integrals takes advantage the freedom to deform the integration contour without changing the integral by Cauchy’s theorem (provided the functions are analytic). If we write
g(t)=\phi(t)+i\psi(t)
where
\phi,\psi
are both real functions, the integral becomes

I(\nu)\sim\int_{\gamma}\differential{t}f(t)e^{\nu(\phi(t)+i\psi(t))}.

(A.58)

We now wish to choose a new integration contour
\gamma^{\prime}
such that along that contour
\psi(t)
is constant, removing the oscillations in the integral

I(\nu)\sim e^{i\nu\psi_{*}}\int_{\gamma^{\prime}}\differential{t}f(t)e^{\nu\phi(t)}.

(A.59)

where
\psi_{*}
is the value of
\psi
along
\gamma^{\prime}
. Then the remaining integral can be approximated via Laplace’s method.

Writing the complex integration variable as
t=x+iy
, the Cauchy-Riemann conditions for
g(t)
to be analytic read

\partialderivative{\phi}{x}=\partialderivative{\psi}{y},\quad\partialderivative{\phi}{y}=-\partialderivative{\psi}{x}\,,

(A.60)

and the functions
\psi
and
\psi
are both harmonic, with
\nabla^{2}\phi=\nabla^{2}\psi=0
. This means that the only critical points of
\phi
which can occur are saddle points (as maxima or minima would require
\partial_{x}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}^{2}\phi>0
).

The contours which have constant
\psi=\mathrm{Im}(g)
must have
\nabla\psi
perpendicular to that contour, and by the Cauchy-Riemann conditions,
\nabla\phi
must then be along said contour. Thus the contours of constant
\psi
are also contours of steepest descent (or ascent) of
\phi
. Thus the method outlined above is usually referred to as the method of steepest descent.

We note briefly also that the “thimbles” of Picard-Lefschetz theory
(e.g.
Feldbrugge et al., 2019
)
are these steepest descent/ascent contours, together with a prescription for how to flow from one contour to another, to find such contours.

A.5
Multi-index notation

Multi-indices make certain results in multivariable calculus much more notationally compact and transparent. A multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
is a list of
N
non-negative integers. We define
size
of a multi-index
\alpha
by

\absolutevalue{\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{N}.

(A.61)

We define the
factorial
of a multi-index,
\alpha!
as the product of the factorials of each index

\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}!=\alpha_{1}!\dots,\alpha_{N}!.

(A.62)

For a vector
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
we define the exponentiation by a multi-index as

\bm{x}^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=0}^{N}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{N}^{\alpha_{N}}.

(A.63)

We define the
\alpha
th
partial derivative in an analogous way, writing

\partial_{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{N}}^{\alpha_{N}}.

(A.64)

With this notation we can write down generalisations to the multinomial and Taylor’s theorems in a very neat and compact way. The multinomial theorem in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
becomes

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{N})^{m}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\alpha}=m}\frac{m!}{\alpha!}(x_{1}+\dots+x_{N})^{\alpha}.

(A.65)

Note that writing this more explicitly

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{N})^{m}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{N}=m}\frac{m!}{\alpha_{1}!\dots\alpha_{N}!}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{N}^{\alpha_{N}},

(A.66)

Taylor’s theorem becomes

f(\bm{x})=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\alpha}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0})^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial^{\alpha}f\evaluated{}_{\xx=\bm{x_0}}.

(A.67)

Appendix B
Further perturbation theory calculations

B.1
Linear growth factor

The linear growth factor can be written in terms of hypergeometric functions (defined in Appendix
A.3
) in the following way.

Linearised density perturbations obey

\ddot{\delta}+2H(t)\dot{\delta}=\frac{3\Omega_{m}(t)H^{2}(t)}{2}\delta,

(B.1)

which defines the growth factor. Letting
\lambda(a)=\Omega_{\Lambda}/\Omega_{m}(a)
,
\lambda_{0}=\lambda(a=0)
, the growing mode solution is
(
Demianski et al., 2005
)

D_{+}(a)=a\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}a^{3}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{6},\frac{11}{6},-\lambda_{0}a^{3}\right),

(B.2)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)
is the Gauss hypergeometric function. The decaying mode decays as

D_{-}(a)=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}a^{3}}a^{-3/2}.

(B.3)

For small scale factors, the growing mode can be expanded as

D_{+}(a)=a-\frac{2}{11}\lambda_{0}a^{3}+\order{a^7}.

(B.4)

This relation can be inverted to write

a(D_{+})=D_{+}+\frac{2}{11}\lambda_{0}D_{+}^{3}+\order{D_+^7}.

(B.5)

B.2
Fluid equations in Fourier space

In this Section calculations are done assuming an Einstein-de Sitter background, such that
D_{+}=a
. This largely follows
Bernardeau et al. (2002)
;
Jain & Bertschinger (1994)
. I will also follow their time variable choices, using often using conformal time rather than coordinate time as is used mostly in the body of this thesis.

The fluid equations in real space are

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}+\frac{1}{a}\bm{\nabla}\cdot[(1+\delta)\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=0,

(B.6a)

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}\bm{u}+(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{\nabla})\bm{u}

\displaystyle=-\gradient\Phi_{N},

(B.6b)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Phi_{N}=\frac{4\pi G\bar{\rho}}{a^{2}}\delta

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\Omega_{m}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\delta,

(B.6c)

so the associated equation for the velocity divergence is

\theta^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}\theta+\frac{3}{2}\Omega_{m}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\delta=-\bm{\nabla}\cdot[(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{\nabla})\bm{u}]

(B.6d)

We want to rewrite these in Fourier space. Fourier transforming the continuity equation

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}(\bm{k})+\theta(\bm{k})=-i\bm{k}\cdot\mathcal{F}[\delta\bm{u}](\bm{k})=-i\bm{k}\cdot(\mathcal{F}[\delta]*\mathcal{F}[\bm{u}])(\bm{k})

(B.7)

where we’ve applied the convolution theorem in the last equality. Writing this out as an integral, an noting that since
\theta=\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u}
, in Fourier space we have
\bm{u}(\bm{k})=-i\bm{k}\theta(\bm{k})/k^{2}
the continuity equation reads

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}(\bm{k})+\theta(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=-i\bm{k}\cdot\int\differential[3]{\kk_1}\delta(\bm{k}-\bm{k}_{1})\theta(\bm{k}_{1})\frac{-i\bm{k}_{1}}{k_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int\differential[3]{\kk_1}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}_{12})\delta(\bm{k}_{2})\theta(\bm{k}_{1})\underbrace{\left(\frac{k_{12}\cdot\bm{k}_{1}}{\bm{k}_{1}^{2}}\right)}_{\alpha(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})}

(B.8)

where the function
\alpha(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})
is called a mode coupling function as it couples together different Fourier modes of
\delta
and
\theta
.

To obtain the Fourier Bernoulli equation, consider Fourier transforming the term

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\bm{\nabla}\cdot((\bm{u}\cdot\bm{\nabla})\bm{u})]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\left[\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\gradient|\bm{u}|^{2}-\bm{u}\times\gradient\times\bm{u}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}u^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}[\nabla^{2}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{u})]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\mathcal{F}[\bm{u}\cdot\bm{u}](\bm{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}k^{2}(\mathcal{F}[\bm{u}]*\mathcal{F}[\bm{u}])(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\int\differential[3]{\kk_1}\differential[3]{\kk_2}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}_{12})\frac{k_{12}^{2}(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}))}{2k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}\theta(\bm{k}_{1})\theta(\bm{k}_{2})

(B.9)

thus the full Bernoulli equation in Fourier space is

\theta^{\prime}(\bm{k})+\mathcal{H}(\tau)\theta(\bm{k})+\frac{3}{2}\Omega_{m}(\tau)\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\delta(\bm{k})=\int\differential[3]{\kk_1}\differential[3]{\kk_2}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}_{12})\underbrace{\frac{k_{12}^{2}(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2})}{2k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}}_{\beta(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})}\theta(\bm{k}_{1})\theta(\bm{k}_{2})

(B.10)

The mode coupling functions then define the perturbation kernels
F_{n},G_{n}
. A recursive relation between them can be found by inserting the perturbative ansatz into the fluid equations and matching perturbative orders, as in
Jain & Bertschinger (1994)
.

B.3
F_{2}
and
\nu_{2}
in SPT

In SPT, the symmetrised perturbation kernel
F_{2}
is

\displaystyle F_{2}^{(s)}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{7}\alpha^{(s)}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})+\frac{2}{7}\beta^{(s)}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{7}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2})}{k_{1}k_{2}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{2}}+\frac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\right)+\frac{2}{7}\frac{(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2})^{2}}{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}.

(B.11)

The angular average of the perturbation kernel (which determines the tree-order skewness) can then be obtained by integrating over the angle between
\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}

\nu_{2}=\frac{2!}{(4\pi)^{2}}\times\int\differential{\Omega_1}\differential{\Omega_2}F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})\,,

(B.12)

where
\differential{\Omega_i}=\sin\theta_{i}\differential{\theta_i}\differential{\phi}_{i}
is the solid angle element. Since
F_{2}
depends only on the angle between the vectors, we change to
\differential{\Omega}\differential{\Omega_{12}}
to obtain

\nu_{2}=\frac{2!}{(4\pi)^{2}}\times\int\differential{\Omega}\times\int\differential{\Omega_{12}}F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}).

(B.13)

Since the angular dependence of
F_{2}
comes from
\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}=k_{1}k_{2}\cos\theta_{12}
, we make use of

\int\differential{\theta}\sin\theta\cos\theta=0,\quad\int\differential{\theta}\sin\theta\cos^{2}\theta=\frac{2}{3}\,,

(B.14)

which means that the angular average of
F_{2}
is

\nu_{2}=2\left[\frac{5}{7}+0+\frac{2}{7}\times\frac{1}{3}\right]=\frac{34}{17}.

(B.15)

B.4
Matching spherical collapse index to skewness

Consider the fitting formula for spherical collapse

\mathcal{F}(\delta_{\rm L})=1+\delta_{\rm NL}^{\rm SC}(\delta_{\rm L})=\left(1-\frac{\delta_{\rm L}}{\nu_{\rm SC}}\right)^{-\nu_{\rm SC}}\,,

(B.16)

which is parametrised by some parameter
\nu_{\rm SC}
. The expansion of this density leads to the following for the skewness

\delta_{\rm NL}=\delta_{\rm L}+\frac{1}{2!}\frac{\nu_{\rm SC}+1}{\nu_{\rm SC}}\delta_{\rm L}^{2}+\frac{1}{3!}\frac{(\nu_{\rm SC}+1)(\nu_{\rm SC}+2)}{\nu_{\rm SC}^{2}}\delta_{\rm L}^{3}+\dots\,,

(B.17)

that is, the
n^{\rm th}
vertex factor for this fitting formula gives:

\nu_{n}=\frac{\nu_{\rm SC}(\nu_{\rm SC}+1)\dots(\nu_{\rm SC}+(n-1))}{\nu_{\rm SC}^{n-1}}\,,

(B.18)

so, the predicted tree-order skewness for this expansion is

S_{3}^{\rm tree,fitting}=3\nu_{2}=3\frac{\nu_{\rm SC}+1}{\nu_{\rm SC}}=3\left(1+\frac{1}{\nu_{\rm SC}}\right).

(B.19)

This means to match the tree-order skewness predicted from SPT by using this fitting formula, we should choose a spherical collapse index
\nu_{\rm SC}
related to the PT vertex
\nu_{2}
by

\nu_{\rm SC}=\frac{1}{\nu_{2}-1}

(B.20)

which in 3D is

\nu_{\rm SC}=\frac{1}{34/21-1}=\frac{21}{13}\approx 1.615.

(B.21)

B.5
Smoothing contribution to skewness

In this Section we present the calculation of the result that the reduced skewness at tree order in SPT, when smoothed with spherical top-hat filter in 3D is
(
Bernardeau, 1994b
)

S_{3}^{\rm tree,SPT}(R)=\frac{34}{7}+\derivative{\log\sigma_R^2}{\log R}.

(B.22)

Anticipating this result we write down a couple of useful things. The variance on scale
R
is given as

\sigma_{R}^{2}=\int\frac{\differential{k}}{2\pi^{2}}k^{2}P(k)W(kR)^{2}.

(B.23)

The log-derivative smoothing term is

\displaystyle\derivative{\log\sigma_R^2}{\log R}

\displaystyle=\frac{R}{\sigma^{2}}\derivative{\sigma^2}{R}

(B.24)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int\frac{\differential{k}}{2\pi^{2}}k^{2}P(k)R\derivative{R}[W(kR)^{2}]

(B.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int\frac{\differential{k}}{2\pi^{2}}k^{2}P(k)\cdot(2kR)\cdot W(kR)W^{\prime}(kR)\,,

(B.26)

where prime here denotes differentiation with respect to the argument.

The ability to write the smoothing term in closed form relies on properties of Bessel functions (which are the Fourier transform of real space spherical smoothing kernels). The identities we will need to use involve the functions

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[1+\frac{\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}}{k_{1}^{2}}\right]\,,

(B.27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[1-\frac{(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2})^{2}}{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}\right]\,,

(B.28)

which arise in the integrals

\displaystyle\int\frac{\differential{\Omega_{12}}}{4\pi}W(\absolutevalue{\bm{k}_1+\bm{k}_2})\mathscr{P}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=W(k_{1})W(k_{2})+\frac{1}{3}k_{2}W^{\prime}(k_{2})W(k_{1})\,,

(B.29)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\differential{\Omega_{12}}}{4\pi}W(\absolutevalue{\bm{k}_1+\bm{k}_2})\mathscr{Q}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}W(k_{1})W(k_{2}).

(B.30)

The (symmetrised) 2nd order SPT density kernel function can be written in terms of these functions

\displaystyle F_{2}^{(s),\rm SPT}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{7}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}}{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{2}}+\frac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\right)+\frac{2}{7}\frac{(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2})^{2}}{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}}

(B.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{P}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})+\mathscr{P}(\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{1})\right]-\frac{2}{7}\mathscr{Q}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})\,,

(B.32)

The tree order skewness corresponds to
\expectationvalue{\delta_R^3(\xx)}=3\expectationvalue{\delta^{(1)}_R(\xx)\delta^{(1)}_R(\xx)\delta^{(2)}_R(\xx)}
as we’ve seen. The second order smoothed density can be written (taking
\bm{x}=0
by homogeneity allows us to drop the exponential in the Fourier transform)

\displaystyle\delta^{(2)}_{R}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=(\delta^{(2)}*W_{R})(\bm{x})=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\delta^{(2)}(\bm{k})W(kR)]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk}\differential[3]{\kk_1}\differential[3]{\kk_2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}_{12})W(kR)\delta_{\rm L}(\bm{k}_{1})\delta_{\rm L}(\bm{k}_{2})F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_1}\differential[3]{\kk_2}}{(2\pi)^{6}}\,W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)\,\delta_{\rm L}(\bm{k}_{1})\,\delta_{\rm L}(\bm{k}_{2})\,F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}).

(B.33)

Inserting this into the tree order skewness we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{R}^{3}\rangle_{c}^{\rm tree}=3\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_{1,2,3,4}}}{(2\pi)^{12}}\,W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)\,W(k_{3}R)\,

\displaystyle W(k_{4}R)\,F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})\times

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_1)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_2)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_3)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_4)}_{c}.

(B.34)

Because of the structure of
F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})
, if we pair
\bm{k}_{1}
and
\bm{k}_{2}
together via Wick’s theorem, the contribution to the integral will vanish. The other terms contribute equally if we use the symmetrised
F_{2}
kernel function, allowing us to write the four point correlation

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_1)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_2)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_3)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_4)}_{c}

\displaystyle=2\expectationvalue{\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_1)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_3)}\expectationvalue{\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_2)\delta_{\rm L}(\kk_4)}

\displaystyle=2\cdot(2\pi)^{6}\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{k}_{2}+\bm{k}_{4})P(k_{1})P(k_{2}).

(B.35)

Putting this in to the skewness integral and integrating out the Dirac deltas leaves

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{R}^{2}\rangle_{c}^{\rm tree}=6\int\frac{\differential[3]{\kk_{1,2}}}{(2\pi)^{6}}\,W(k_{1}R)\,P_{\rm L}(k_{1})\,W(k_{2}R)\,P_{\rm L}(k_{2})\,W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)\,F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})

(B.36)

We now change to polar coordinates,
\differential[3]{\kk}_{i}=k_{i}^{2}\differential{k}_{i}\differential{\Omega}_{i}
. Note as well that since
F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})
and
W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)
depend only on the relative angle between
\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}
we can do one of the angular integrals immediately to obtain
4\pi
, and then only integrate only over the angle between
\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{R}^{2}\rangle_{c}^{\rm tree}=6\int\frac{\differential{k_1}\differential{k_2}}{(2\pi)^{6}}\,

\displaystyle k_{1}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{1})W(k_{1}R)\,k_{2}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{2})W(k_{2}R)\times

\displaystyle 4\pi\int\differential{\Omega_{12}}W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)\,F_{2}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}).

(B.37)

We now make use of the properties of the window function to solve the angular part of this integral. Defining the angular part

\displaystyle I=(4\pi)^{2}\int\differential{\Omega_{12}}W(\absolutevalue{\kk_1+\kk_2}R)\left[\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})-\frac{2}{7}\mathscr{Q}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2})\right]\,,

(B.38)

and applying the identities (
B.29
)

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{2}\times\left[\frac{17}{21}W(k_{1}R)W(k_{2}R)+\frac{1}{6}k_{1}RW^{\prime}(k_{1}R)W(k_{2}R)+\frac{1}{6}k_{2}RW^{\prime}(k_{2}R)W(k_{1}R)\right].

The full skewness integral then becomes

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{R}^{3}\rangle_{c}^{\rm tree}=

\displaystyle\phantom{+}\frac{34}{17}\int\frac{\differential{k_1}}{2\pi^{2}}k_{1}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{1})W^{2}(k_{1}R)\int\frac{\differential{k_2}}{2\pi^{2}}k_{2}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{2})W^{2}(k_{2}R)

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\differential{k_1}}{2\pi^{2}}\,k_{1}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{1})W(k_{1}R)(k_{1}R)W^{\prime}(k_{1}R)\int\frac{\differential{k_2}}{2\pi^{2}}k_{2}^{2}P_{\rm L}(k_{2})W^{2}(k_{2}R)+(k_{1}\leftrightarrow k_{2})\,,

which we can simplify by recognising the linear variance and the derivative of the linear variance (noting we can simply double the second line to account for the symmetrisation)

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{R}^{3}\rangle_{c}^{\rm tree}

\displaystyle=\frac{34}{17}(\sigma_{R}^{2})^{2}+\sigma_{R}^{2}R\derivative{ \sigma_R^2}{ R}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{R}^{2})^{2}\left[\frac{34}{17}+\frac{R}{\sigma^{2}_{R}}\derivative{ \sigma_R^2}{ R}\right]\,,

(B.39)

which reproduces the tree order reduced skewness

S_{3}^{\rm tree,\,SPT}=\frac{34}{17}+\derivative{\log\sigma_R^2}{\log R}.

(B.40)

Appendix C
Appendix for Chapter
6

C.1
Deriving the auxillary Laplace transform in the minimal tree model

Here we calculate the auxiliary version of the Laplace transform of the CGF as the integral
I_{j}(\rho)
used in Chapter
6
to obtain the PDF and bias functions for the minimal tree model. This calculation was presented in
Uhlemann et al., 2023
, but here contains a correction to correctly derive the Dirac delta term of the PDF. The integral of interest is:

I_{j}(\rho)=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\differential{\lambda}}{2\pi i}\exp\left[{-\lambda\rho+j\phi(\lambda)}\right].

(C.1)

The PDF is then the value
I_{1}(\rho)
, and the bias functions for the minimal tree model are obtained by successive derivatives with respect to
j
,

b_{n,\rm t}(\rho)\mathcal{P}_{\rm t}(\rho)=\derivative[n]{I_j(\rho)}{j}\evaluated{}_{j=1}.

(C.2)

The minimal tree one-point CGF is

\phi(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\sigma^{2}/2}\,,

(C.3)

which we can insert into the integral. Recall that if a complex function
f(z)
has a pole of order
n
at
z=c
, the residue associated with that pole can be obtained by

\mathrm{Res}_{f}(c)=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\lim_{z\to c}\derivative[n-1]{z}\left[(z-c)^{n}f(z)\right].

(C.4)

Motivated by this we rewrite the one-point CGF as

\phi(\lambda)=\frac{-2\lambda/\sigma^{2}}{\lambda-2/\sigma^{2}}\,,

(C.5)

and we then can expand the exponential as a series in

e^{j\phi(\lambda)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(j\phi)^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(-\frac{2j\lambda}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n}\frac{1}{(\lambda-2/\sigma^{2})^{n}}.

(C.6)

The
n=0
term does not have a simple pole, and takes the form

\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\differential{\lambda}}{2\pi i}e^{-\lambda\rho}\,,

(C.7)

which is simply a Dirac delta term
\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)
(consider
\lambda\to-i\lambda
and compare to the Fourier representations of the Dirac delta in Appendix
A.1
). The total integral is thus (after exchanging the sum and integral)

I_{j}(\rho)=\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int\differential{\lambda}e^{-\lambda\rho}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{2j\lambda}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n}\frac{1}{(\lambda-2/\sigma^{2})^{n}}.

(C.8)

Choose the integration contour to be up the imaginary
\lambda
axis and closing towards the positive real axis since the pole is at
(2/\sigma^{2})^{n}
. Along this contour we can apply Cauchy’s residue theorem to each term in this sum (calling the integrand
f_{n}
for brevity)

I_{j}(\rho)=\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\differential{\lambda}f_{n}(\lambda)=\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Res}_{f_{n}}(2/\sigma^{2}).

(C.9)

To calculate the
n^{\rm th}
residue we need the
(n-1)^{\rm th}
derivative of the product appearing in this sum. We make use of

\displaystyle\derivative[k]{\lambda}e^{-\lambda\rho}

\displaystyle=(-\rho)^{k}e^{-\lambda\rho}\,,

(C.10a)

\displaystyle\derivative[k]{\lambda}\left[\left(-\frac{2j}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n}\lambda^{n}\right]=\left(-\frac{2j}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n}\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}\lambda^{n-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}\left(-\frac{2j}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{k}\left(-\frac{2j\lambda}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n-k}\,,

(C.10b)

then apply the generalised product rule,

\derivative[n-1]{\lambda}\left[g(\lambda)h(\lambda)\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k}\derivative[k]{g}{\lambda}\derivative[n-k]{h}{\lambda}\,,

(C.11)

to obtain the
n^{\rm th}
residue at
\lambda=2/\sigma^{2}
, we obtain

\text{Res}_{f_{n}}(2/\sigma^{2})=e^{-2\rho/\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{k!(k+1)!(n-k-1)!}(-\rho)^{k}\left(-\frac{2j}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n-1-k}\left(-\frac{4j}{\sigma^{4}}\right)^{k+1}.

(C.12)

Putting this into the sum of residuals and shifting the sum of residuals to start at
n=0
the resulting double sum can be factored as a product via Cauchy sums

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\text{Res}_{f_{n}}(2/\sigma^{2})=-\frac{4j}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-2\rho/\sigma^{2}}\left[\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+1)!}\left(\frac{4j\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right)^{\ell}\right]\left[\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r!}\left(-\frac{2j}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{r}\right].

(C.13)

We can resum these sums in square brackets using hypergeometric functions, so we finally arrive at

I_{j}(\rho)=\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)+\frac{4j}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}(j+\rho)}{}_{0}F_{1}\left(2,\frac{4j\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right).

(C.14)

To obtain the PDF, we need to evaluate
I_{j}
at
j=1
and ensure the PDF is appropriately normalised. We do this by introducing a coefficient in front of the Dirac delta term such that the integral over the entire PDF over the range
\rho\in[0,\infty)
is equal to 1. The continuous part of the PDF obtained by taking the continuous part of
I_{j=1}(\rho)
,

\mathcal{P}_{\rm continuous}(\rho)=\frac{4}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}(1+\rho)}{}_{0}F_{1}\left(2,\frac{4\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right).

(C.15)

The integral over the continuous part of the PDF is given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{\rm continuous}(\rho)\differential{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}{}_{0}F_{1}\left(2,\frac{4\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\differential{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\Gamma(n+2)\Gamma(n+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\rho/\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{4\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right)^{n}\differential{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2/\sigma^{2})^{n}}{\Gamma(n+2)\Gamma(n+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\rho/\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{2\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{n}\differential\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2/\sigma^{2})^{n}}{(n+1)!n!}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}x^{n}\differential{x}

\displaystyle=e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2/\sigma^{2})^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}

(C.16)

where we have integrated the series expansion of the hypergeometric function term by term. We then recognise that we can rewrite the sum as

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2/\sigma^{2})^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2/\sigma^{2})^{n}}{(n)!}-1=e^{2/\sigma^{2}}-1

(C.17)

such that the integral over the continuous part of the PDF is simply
1-e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}
. To make the integral over the total PDF equal to 1, we introduce a factor
e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}
, such that the final PDF in the minimal tree model is given by

\mathcal{P}(\rho)=e^{-2/\sigma^{2}}\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)+\frac{4}{\sigma^{4}}e^{-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}(1+\rho)}{}_{0}F_{1}\left(2,\frac{4\rho}{\sigma^{4}}\right).

(C.18)

The bias functions are obtained by derivatives as described in Chapter
6
.

We note here that a pair of identities presented in Appendix C of
Bernardeau & Pichon, 2024
which can be used to derive this PDF more quickly but were unknown at the time of writing
Uhlemann et al., 2023
. They are

\int\frac{\differential{\lambda}}{2\pi i}\exp(-\lambda\rho+ \frac{A}{\lambda_c - \lambda})=\delta_{\rm D}(\rho)+Ae^{-\lambda_{c}\rho}{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(2;A\rho),

(C.19)

and

\int\frac{\differential{\lambda}}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{c}-\lambda}\right)^{n}\exp(-\lambda\rho+ \frac{A}{\lambda_c - \lambda})=e^{-\lambda_{c}\rho}\left(\derivative{A}\right)^{n}[A\,{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(2;A\rho)],

(C.20)

for
n\geq 1
. Here
{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(a;z)
is the regularised confluent hypergeometric function defined as
{}_{0}\tilde{F}_{1}(a;z)={}_{0}F_{1}(a;z)/\Gamma(a)
. Since in our case we are interested in
\tilde{F}_{1}(2;A\rho)
and
\Gamma(2)=1
, we can simply replace the regularised hypergeometric function with the ordinary one. This identity immediately leads to the form of the PDF, and differentiation with respect to
A
obtains the bias functions. One still has to do the integration over the continuous part to obtain the prefactor for the Dirac delta term.

C.2
Shifted lognormal model

Here we detail the specific shifted lognormal model used in
Uhlemann et al., 2023
in the context of the weak lensing convergence
\kappa
and the matter density contrast
\delta
.

A bivariate shifted lognormal joint PDF for the density contrast
\delta
is parametrised by the variance
\sigma_{\delta}^{2}
and the two-point correlation function
\xi_{\delta}=\xi_{12,\delta}(d)
between the cells, and a shift parameter
s
related to the skewness, which we will keep fixed. We define a Gaussian-distributed variable
g(\delta)
with zero-mean

g(\delta)=\ln(\frac{\delta}{s}+1)-\mu_{\rm G}=\ln(\frac{\delta}{s}+1)+\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}\,,

(C.21)

where
\mu_{\rm G}=-\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}/2
is fixed to ensure that
\expectationvalue{\delta}=0
and the Gaussian variance
\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}=\ln(1+\sigma_\delta^2/2)
. This can then be used to construct the shifted lognormal univariate PDF

\mathcal{P}_{\rm LN}(\delta;\sigma_{\rm G}^{2},s)=\frac{\Theta(\delta+s)}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{\rm G}(\delta+s)}\exp[-\frac{g(\delta)^{2}}{2\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}}]\,,

(C.22)

where
\Theta
is the Heavyside step function such that the PDF is set to zero for
\delta\leqslant-s
. The shift parameter
s
can be set to produce the desired skewness of the PDF
(see the formula for
\lambda
in
Xavier et al., 2016
)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{\delta}}{\tilde{\mu}_{3}}\left(1+y(\tilde{\mu}_{3})^{-1}+y(\tilde{\mu}_{3})\right)-\langle\delta\rangle\approx 3\frac{\sigma_{\delta}^{4}}{\langle\delta^{3}\rangle},

(C.23)

\displaystyle y(\tilde{\mu}_{3})

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{2+\tilde{\mu}_{3}^{2}+\tilde{\mu}_{3}\sqrt{4+\tilde{\mu}_{3}^{2}}}{2}\right]^{1/3}\approx 1\,,

where
\langle\delta\rangle
is the desired mean,
\sigma
the target variance and
\tilde{\mu}_{3}=\langle\delta^{3}\rangle/\sigma_{\delta}^{3}
the skewness. The Gaussian limit is reproduced by sending
s/\sigma_{\delta}^{2}\to\infty
. The standard lognormal model
(e.g.
Coles & Jones, 1991
)
is recovered for
s=1
.

The bivariate shifted lognormal model is then expressed in terms of the correlation
\xi_{\rm G}=\ln(1+\xi_\delta/s^2)
and the determinant
D=\sigma_{\rm G}^{4}-\xi_{\rm G}^{2}
as

\mathcal{P}_{\rm LN}(\delta_{1},\delta_{2};\sigma_{\rm G}^{2},\xi_{\rm G},s)=\frac{\Theta(\delta_{1}+s)\Theta(\delta_{2}+s)}{2\pi\sqrt{D}(\delta_{1}+s)(\delta_{2}+s)}\exp[-\frac{\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}}{2}(g(\delta_{1})^{2}+g(\delta_{2})^{2})+\frac{\xi_{\rm G}}{D}g(\delta_{1})g(\delta_{2})].

(C.24)

The leading order and NLO bias functions for the shifted lognormal model cannot be obtained in the same manner as presented in Chapter
6
, as it is not well described in terms of a CGF. However, since the two-point PDF is expressible in closed form we can instead simply do a brute force expansion in powers of the correlation function to obtain bias functions from this PDF. This requires an extra step compared to simply expanding the PDF in powers of
\xi_{\rm G}
as the Gaussian field correlation terms
\xi_{\rm G}
must also be expanded in terms of the underlying
\delta
correlation
\xi_{\delta}
. To next-to-leading order, this expansion produces (in the
\xi_{\rm G}(\xi_{\delta})/\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}\ll 1
limit)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\rm LN}(\delta_{1},\delta_{2};\xi_{\delta})}{\mathcal{P}_{\rm LN}(\delta_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\rm LN}(\delta_{2})}=

\displaystyle\phantom{+}1+\left(\frac{\xi_{\delta}}{s^{2}}-\frac{\xi_{\delta}^{2}}{2s^{4}}\right)\frac{g(\delta_{1})}{\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}}\frac{g(\delta_{2})}{\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}}

(C.25a)

\displaystyle+\frac{\xi_{\delta}^{2}}{2s^{4}}\frac{\left(g(\delta_{1})^{2}-\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}\right)}{\sigma_{\rm G}^{4}}\frac{\left(g(\delta_{2})^{2}-\sigma_{\rm G}^{2}\right)}{\sigma_{\rm G}^{4}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\xi_{\delta}^{3}).

In principle, on can obtain the bias results to any order for this model using results for the Gaussian bias functions at arbitrary order.

Appendix D
Appendix for Chapter
7

D.1
Domain colouring

We represent complex numbers,
z=\absolutevalue{z}e^{i\theta}
, with the argument,
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, as points in hue, lightness, saturation (hls) colour space via the mapping

\begin{pmatrix}h\\
l\\
s\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta/(2\pi)\\
1-0.5^{\absolutevalue{z}}\\
0.5\end{pmatrix}.

(D.1)

Each of
\{h,l,s\}
lies in the range
0
to
1
. These hls values can then be mapped to standard RGB values (or any other colour space) in the usual way (c.f.
Wegert, 2012
).

D.2
Derivation of the hidden phase (following Fried 1998)

Fried, 1998
derives an explicit expression for the hidden phase based on the location of the branch points in an optical field (equation (
D.12
)). Such a phase profile could be applicable to studying constant time snapshots for a 2-dimensional system, where now the branch points act as vortex cores
(as shown in
Uhlemann et al., 2019
)
. Below we present the derivation of this hidden phase from this work.

The wavefunction
\psi
plays the role of the optical field, while the velocity field
\bm{v}
velocity field plays the role of the “gradient field” of the principal valued phase of the optical field,
\bm{g}
.

Consider a 2-dimensional velocity field,
\bm{v}
, with a single branch point located at
\bm{r}_{\rm BP}
with a circulation of
\pm 2\pi
as defined by equation (
7.36
). We guarantee that the circulation is always
\pm 2\pi
and not some other integer multiple by constructing it from the “principal valued” phase function. By introducing an auxiliary third dimension,
z
, we can write the circulation integral as the condition,

\hat{\bm{e}}_{z}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{v}(\bm{r})=\pm 2\pi\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{r}-\bm{r}_{\rm BP})\,.

(D.2)

Performing a Helmholtz decomposition on the velocity field, we obtain the incompressible and irrotational parts of the velocity

\bm{v}=\bm{\nabla}\phi_{s}+\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{H}\,.

(D.3)

where now
\phi_{s}
and
\bm{H}
are smooth functions, in contrast to the raw phase which describes this (optical) field, which contains jumps from the branch points.

The scalar potential,
\phi_{s}
, determines the divergence of the velocity field,

\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{v}=\nabla^{2}\phi_{s}\,.

(D.4)

For our 2-dimensional system, we can write the vector potential as a scalar potential in the auxiliary dimension,

\bm{H}(\bm{r})=h(\bm{r})\hat{\bm{e}}_{z}\,.

(D.5)

Since the divergence of
\bm{H}
vanishes (as it is solenoidal), we can rewrite the condition in equation (
D.2
) as

\nabla^{2}h(\bm{r})=\mp 2\pi\delta_{\rm D}(\bm{r}-\bm{r}_{\rm BP})\,,

(D.6)

which is solved by

h(\bm{r})=\mp\log(\abs{\bm{r}-\bm{r}_{\rm BP}})\,.

(D.7)

To then write velocity field as the gradient of some (principal valued) velocity potential
\bm{v}=\bm{\nabla}\phi
with some split into a “smooth” and a “hidden” part,
\phi=\phi_{\rm sm}+\phi_{\rm hid}
, we make the choice that
\phi_{\rm sm}
should be equal to the scalar potential
\phi_{s}
from the Helmholtz decomposition. Then, the equation for the hidden phase

\bm{\nabla}\phi_{\rm hid}=\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{H}\,,

(D.8)

becomes the pair of equations

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\phi_{\rm hid}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{y}[-h(x,y)]\,,

(D.9a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\phi_{\rm hid}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[-h(x,y)]\,,

(D.9b)

which can be interpreted as the Cauchy-Riemann conditions for a single complex analytic function

f(z=x+iy)=-h(x,y)+i\phi_{\rm hid}(x,y)\,,

(D.10)

with real part
-h
and imaginary part
\phi_{\rm hid}
. Using the expression for
h(\bm{r})
in equation (
D.7
), the hidden phase for a single branch point is

\phi_{\rm hid}(\bm{r})=\imaginary\{\pm\log\left[(x-x_{\rm BP}+i(y-y_{\rm BP})\right]\}.

(D.11)

If instead the system contains multiple branch points, each will contribute a term of this form to the overall hidden phase. Since we are concerned with irrotational systems, these branch points must be created in pairs such that the number of positive and negative branch points is equal. Consider
N
pairs of branch points, with the
i^{\rm th}
point associated with circulation
\pm 2\pi
located at
\bm{r}_{i}^{\pm}=(x_{i}^{\pm},y_{i}^{\pm})
. The total hidden phase from these branch points is then

\phi_{\rm hid}(\bm{r})=\imaginary\left\{\log\left[\frac{\prod\limits_{n=1}^{N}(x-x_{n}^{+})+i(y-y_{n}^{+})}{\prod\limits_{m=1}^{N}(x-x_{m}^{-})+i(y-y_{m}^{-})}\right]\right\}.

(D.12)

D.3
Contribution of the fold to the cusp diffraction integral

Away from the singular cusp point, the cusp catastrophe unfolds into a pair of fold lines. On lines of constant
C_{2}<0
, near to the classical caustic line, the integral is dominated by the contribution from the folded part of the phase-space sheet. This section demonstrates how to obtain the contribution from the fold to the cusp integral on these constant
C_{2}
lines.
Start with the cusp diffraction integral

\displaystyle u_{\rm cusp}(C_{1},C_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\nu}{2\pi}}\int\differential{s}\exp(i\nu\zeta_{\rm cusp}(s;C_1,C_2))\,,

(D.13a)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\rm cusp}(s;C_{1},C_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{4}}{4}+C_{2}\frac{s^{2}}{2}+C_{1}s\,.

(D.13b)

Consider a horizontal slice through this integral at constant
C_{2}<0
. We want to evaluate the contribution along this line near the caustic line described by equation (
7.59
). On the actual caustic line, there are 2 stationary points of
\zeta
, one single and one double root. We are interested in the double root, which produces the fold. The stationary points along the caustic line satisfy:

s_{*}^{3}+C_{2}s_{*}+\left(\frac{4}{27}\absolutevalue{C_2}^{3}\right)^{1/2}=0\,,

(D.14)

where we have written
C_{1}=C_{1}(C_{2})
using equation (
7.59
). This has two solutions in
s
,

s_{*}=\sqrt{\frac{-C_{2}}{3}},-2\sqrt{\frac{-C_{2}}{3}}\,.

(D.15)

The positive stationary point is the double root if
C_{1}>0
, and the other is if
C_{1}<0
. Generically, call the double root
s_{2}
. Change coordinates in the integral
t=s-s_{2}
, so that the fold occurs at
t=0
. Under this transformation, the cusp integral has the form

u_{\rm cusp}=\sqrt{\frac{\nu}{2\pi}}\int\differential{t}\exp(i\nu\left[\frac{t^4}{4}+At^3 + Bt^2 + C t + D\right]),

(D.16)

(we will not list explicit forms of all intermediate variables, as this section is simply to show that such a set of transformations can be done). Now we want to remove the
\nu
dependence in a way which lets us analyse the fold contribution to this integral coming from
t=0
. Under the coordinate change
t=y/(3A\nu)^{1/3}
the cubic term will become
y^{3}/3
resembling the fold integral. The resulting integral is

\displaystyle u_{\rm cusp}=\sqrt{\frac{\nu}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(3A\nu)^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\int\differential{y}\exp(i\frac{\nu^{-1/3}}{4(3A)^{4/3}}y^4)\times

\displaystyle\exp(i\left[\frac{y^3}{3} + \tilde{B}y^2 + \tilde{C} y + \tilde{D}\right]).

(D.17)

We then notice that the quartic term is suppressed in the
\nu\to\infty
limit, and we can expand the exponential

\displaystyle\exp(i\frac{\nu^{-1/3}}{4(3A)^{4/3}}y^4)\sim 1+\order{\nu^{-1/3}}.

(D.18)

So to leading order in
\nu
we can neglect the quartic term’s contribution to the integral in equation (
D.17
) at
y=0
. Note that this is the same trick used in standard stationary phase analysis, but here we retain the cubic term in the exponent, where in stationary phase we would expand all exponential terms of cubic or higher order and then perform the resulting Gaussian integrals.

We can turn the resulting equation into the standard fold integral by taking
y=x-\tilde{B}
, which will transform this cubic into a depressed cubic (one without a quadratic term). This results in the contribution

\displaystyle\hat{u}=\frac{\nu^{1/6}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(3A)^{1/3}}\int\differential{x}\exp\left(i\left[\frac{x^{3}}{3}+Ex+F\right]\right),

(D.19)

to the cusp integral. We have written
\hat{u}
here rather than
u_{\rm cusp}
to remind us that this is an approximation to the contribution only around
s=s_{2}
, rather than a full analysis of the cusp integral. This fold contribution
\hat{u}
is now in the form of the standard fold diffraction integral

\displaystyle\hat{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{iF}\nu^{1/6}}{(3A)^{1/3}}u_{\rm fold}(E;\nu=1)\,,

(D.20)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{e^{iF}}{(3A)^{1/3}}\nu^{1/6}\operatorname{Ai}(E)\,.

(D.21)

The explicit forms of
E
and
F
are

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\nu^{2/3}\frac{3^{2/3}\left(12C_{1}s_{2}-C_{2}^{2}+3s_{2}^{2}\left(2C_{2}+s_{2}^{2}\right)\right)}{36s_{2}^{4/3}}\,,

(D.22)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\nu\frac{\left(-18C_{1}C_{2}s_{2}+C_{2}^{3}+9s_{2}^{2}\left(6C_{1}s_{2}-C_{2}^{2}+C_{2}s_{2}^{2}\right)\right)}{108s_{2}^{2}}\,.

(D.23)

Notice that
E\sim\nu^{2/3}
, correctly recovering the fringe index for the fold catastrophe.

One could perform a similar analysis on the full wavefunction with cosine initial conditions (
7.60
) on constant time slices post shell crossing. Doing this would result in a set of coordinate relations between
(x,a)
and the single control parameter of the standard fold integral. Doing this explicitly is significantly messier than the case here, requiring an expansion of cosine around the caustic line described in equation (
7.9
). Additionally, the wavefunction contribution cannot be written in closed form since the solutions
s_{2}
cannot be written down analytically (which was only possible here because
\zeta_{\rm cusp}^{\prime}=0
is a cubic equation).

D.4
A brief note on
\star
-products

The treatment of quantum mechanics in phase-space is significantly less well known than the usual Schrödinger or Heisenberg pictures of quantum mechanics.
Case (2008)
is a good introduction to the Wigner function and associated Weyl transform.

For readers who wish to go deeper into the underlying mathematical structure of quantum mechanics in phase-space, the introduction of Wigner-Weyl quantisation in
Mari\˜no (2021)
provides motivation for why the Wigner function has the specific form that it does. It begins by motivating the Weyl transform of a function
W(f)
as a kind of non-commutative Fourier transform, then finding a binary operation
\star
, called the
\star
-product (pronounced “star product”)

f\star g=f\exp(\frac{i\hbar}{2}\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\Lambda})g,\quad\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\Lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{\partial}_{x}\overset{\rightarrow}{\partial}_{x}-\overset{\leftarrow}{\partial}_{p}\overset{\rightarrow}{\partial}_{x}\,,

(D.24)

which obeys a kind of “convolution theorem” relation with Weyl transforms analogously to ordinary function convolution with Fourier transforms,

W(f)W(g)=W(f\star g).

(D.25)

The Moyal bracket is then defined as the commutator relative to this
\star
-product, rather than ordinary products

\{f,g\}_{\rm MB}^{\hbar}=[f,g]_{\star}=f\star g-g\star f.

(D.26)

The Wigner function in general is defined as the Weyl transform of the density matrix
\hat{\rho}
, which can encode states with statistical uncertainty as well as quantum uncertainty. For a pure state, this density matrix is
\hat{\rho}_{\psi}=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
. The evolution equation from the Heisenberg picture of quantum mechanics

i\hbar\partialderivative{\hat\rho_\psi}{t}=[\mathscr{H},\hat{\rho}_{\psi}]\,,

(D.27)

with a Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
can then be lifted to phase-space by Wigner transforming both sides, leaving

i\hbar\partialderivative{f_W}{t}=[\mathscr{H},f_{W}]_{\star}=\{\mathscr{H},f_{W}\}_{\rm MB}^{\hbar}.

(D.28)

Curtright & et al. (2013)
and
Curtright & Zachos (2011)
provide more details and a guide to the literature on this subject.

D.5
Deriving the Wigner equation

This Section makes explicit the derivation of the time evolution of the Wigner distribution and compares it to the time evolution of a phase-space distribution obeying the usual Vlasov equation. This can be done more elegantly using operator notation or the
\star
-algebra formulation, but here we present a more elementary and explicit derivation here.

Begin with the definition of the Wigner distribution function (here using the standard momentum variable which is conjugate to position with respect to coordinate time)

f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p},t)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2).

(D.29)

The wavefunctions appearing here obey the Schrödinger equation in expanding coordinates

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\psi}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar}{2ma^{2}}\nabla^{2}\psi-i\frac{m}{\hbar}V\psi\,,

(D.30a)

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\psi^*}{t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\hbar}{2ma^{2}}\nabla^{2}\psi^{*}+i\frac{m}{\hbar}V\psi^{*},

(D.30b)

with some potential
V
.

For the time being, let us rescale
\hbar
to absorb the factor of
m
. We can then directly calculate the time derivative of the Wigner distribution function (we will also omit arguments of functions where they are not needed for brevity)

\partial_{t}f_{W}=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\left[\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\partial_{t}\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)+\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)\partial_{t}\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\right]\,,

(D.31)

now apply the Schrödinger equation, grouping kinetic terms and potential terms together

\partial_{t}f_{W}=I_{T}+I_{V}\,,

(D.32)

where
I_{T}
is the kinetic part of the integral, and
I_{V}
is the potential part

\displaystyle I_{T}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\frac{i\hbar}{2a^{2}}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\left[\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\partial_{x}^{2}\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)+\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)\partial_{x}^{2}\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\right]\,,

(D.33a)

\displaystyle I_{V}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\frac{i}{\hbar}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\left[V(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)-V(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)\right]\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y}/2)\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}/2)\,,

(D.33b)

It will prove slightly notationally easier to work with a different form of the Wigner function, where we redefine our integration variable
\bm{y}\mapsto 2\bm{y}
. In these variables we then have that the Wigner function is

f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})=\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}).

(D.34)

Kinetic term

For brevity, let
\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\pm}=\bm{x}\pm\bm{y}
and functions
F(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\pm})=F_{\pm}
.

For the kinetic term, we want to massage this into something which looks like the Vlasov term
\bm{p}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}}f_{W}
. To do this, we not that we want to have just single
x
derivatives acting on the wave functions, rather than double derivatives. This suggest doing some sort of integration by parts. To do this, we note the useful relation which allows us to swap
x
and
y
derivatives

\partial_{x}F(x\pm y)=\pm\partial_{y}F(x\pm y).

(D.35)

Using this identity to replace one of the
\partial_{x}
in the kinetic term we can then preform integration by parts on
y
.

\displaystyle I_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar}{2a^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\left[\psi^{*}_{+}\partial_{x}^{2}\psi_{-}-\psi_{-}\partial_{x}^{2}\psi_{+}\right]

(D.36)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar}{2a^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\left[-\psi^{*}_{+}\partial_{y}\partial_{x}\psi_{-}-\psi_{-}\partial_{y}\partial_{x}\psi_{+}\right]\,,

(D.37)

We can now integrate by parts with respect to
\bm{y}
. The boundary terms will be of the form
e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi^{(*)}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\pm})\partial_{x}\psi^{(*)}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mp})
which will vanish at infinity due to the vanishing of both the wavefunction and its derivative. Swapping the derivative term we then obtain by parts (notice the sign change from parts)

I_{T}=\frac{i\hbar}{2a^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}\left[\partial_{x}\psi^{*}_{+}\partial_{y}\left(e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi_{-}\right)+\partial_{x}\psi_{-}\partial_{y}\left(e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi^{*}_{+}\right)\right]\,,

(D.38)

\displaystyle I_{T}=\frac{i\hbar}{2a^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\frac{2i\bm{p}}{\hbar}\left(\psi_{-}\partial_{x}\psi^{*}_{+}+\psi^{*}_{+}\partial_{x}\psi_{-}\right)

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}\psi^{*}_{+}\partial_{y}\psi_{-}+\partial_{x}\psi_{-}\partial_{y}\psi^{*}_{+}\Bigg\}.

(D.39)

Notice that the term on the second line of Equation
D.39
vanishes since
\partial_{y}\psi_{-}=-\partial_{x}\psi_{-}
and
\partial_{y}\psi^{*}_{+}=\partial_{x}\psi^{*}_{+}
. We note that the term in the first line of Equation
D.39
is simply the product rule on
\psi_{-}\psi^{*}_{+}
. This means we can write the kinetic term as

I_{T}=-\frac{\bm{p}}{a^{2}}\partial_{x}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{3}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\psi_{-}\psi^{*}_{+}\right]\,,

(D.40)

which leads us to the final form (restoring factors of
m
)

I_{T}=-\frac{\bm{p}}{ma^{2}}\partial_{x}f_{W}\,,

(D.41)

which is precisely of the form of the kinetic term in the Vlasov equation.

Potential term

For the potential term we will make use of the same form of the Wigner function as above.

I_{V}=\frac{1}{(\pi\hbar)^{3}}\frac{i}{\hbar}\int\differential[]\bm{y}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{x}/\hbar}\left[V(\bm{x}+\bm{y})-V(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\right]\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y})

(D.42)

We’re hoping that this turns into something like
V(x)\partial_{p}f_{W}
, so we need to Taylor expand the difference in potentials to get it entirely in terms of
\bm{x}
. To do this we use the multi-index version of Taylor’s theorem (see Section
A.5
). Focusing on the difference in potentials we obtain

V(\bm{x}+\bm{y})-V(\bm{x}-\bm{y})=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\alpha}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}V\evaluated{}_{\bm{x}}\left[\bm{y}^{\alpha}-(-\bm{y})^{\alpha}\right].

(D.43)

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
. Note that when the multi-index has an even magnitude the terms in the Taylor expansion vanish, so we are left summing only odd terms. The potential term is then

I_{V}=\frac{2i}{(\pi\hbar)^{3}\hbar}\sum_{\absolutevalue{k}=0}^{\infty}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}}\frac{\bm{y}^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}\partial_{x}^{2k+1}V(\bm{x})\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y}).

(D.44)

Now we notice that we can write the
\bm{y}^{n}
terms as successive derivatives of the exponential part with respect to
\bm{p}
. Using the identity

\bm{y}^{n}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}=\left(\frac{\hbar}{2i}\right)^{n}\partial_{p}^{n}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}/\hbar}\,,

(D.45)

and noting that
\left(\frac{\hbar}{2i}\right)^{2k+1}=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{i}\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\right)^{2k+1}
, we can write the potential term as

\displaystyle I_{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\hbar}\sum_{\absolutevalue{k}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{\partial_{x}^{2k+1}V(\bm{x})}{(2k+1)!}\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\right)^{2k+1}\underbrace{\frac{1}{(\pi\hbar)^{3}}\int\differential[3]{\bm{y}}\partial_{p}^{2k+1}e^{2i\bm{p}\cdot\bm{y}}\psi(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\psi^{*}(\bm{x}+\bm{y})}_{\partial_{p}^{2k+1}f_{W}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\hbar}\sum_{\absolutevalue{k}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{\partial_{x}^{2k+1}V(\bm{x})}{(2k+1)!}\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\right)^{2k+1}\partial_{p}^{2k+1}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})\,,

(D.46)

Now notice that we are summing an (operator) series of the form
(-1)^{k}Z^{2k+1}/(2k+1)!
which is the series for
\sin(Z)
. We can make this clearer by “peeling off” the functions
V(\bm{x})
and
f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})
. We use
\vec{\partial}
to denote derivatives acting to their right, and

\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}

to denote derivatives acting on all functions appearing on their left. Therefore we have

\displaystyle I_{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\hbar}V(\bm{x})\left(\sum_{\absolutevalue{k}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k+1)!}\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\right)^{2k+1}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}^{2k+1}\vec{\partial}_{p}^{2k+1}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\hbar}V(\bm{x})\sin\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}\vec{\partial}_{p}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})\,,

(D.47)

Putting it all together

The full time evolution of the Wigner distribution function is then the sum of this kinetic and potential term. Using the results from Equation
D.41
and
D.47
we then have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})

\displaystyle=I_{T}+I_{V}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bm{p}}{ma^{2}}\vec{\partial}_{x}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})+mV(\bm{x})\cdot\frac{2}{\hbar}\sin\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}\vec{\partial}_{p}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p}).

(D.48)

Note that if one formally expands the sine function, we recover the Vlasov equation to
\order{\hbar^2}
. As a last manipulation, it would be nice to recast this evolution equation in something like a Hamiltonian form with a bracket on phase-space. To do this we need to write something like the Hamiltonian
\frac{p^{2}}{ma^{2}}+mV
, and we should expect that the sine of the operators should appear in the first term. We can do this by writing the linear
\bm{p}
term as

\bm{p}=\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sin(\cev{\del}_p)\,,

(D.49)

since the lowest order term in the expansion of the
\sin
gives us the linear term in
\bm{p}
and all higher derivatives vanish. We therefore have

\partial_{t}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})=\frac{p^{2}}{2ma^{2}}\sin\left(-\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{p}\right)\vec{\partial}_{x}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})+mV(\bm{x})\cdot\frac{2}{\hbar}\sin\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}\vec{\partial}_{p}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p}).

(D.50)

As each
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{p}
acting on
p^{2}
gives us 0 beyond leading order, we are free to include the
\vec{\partial}_{x}
inside the sine function as any higher term will be 0 anyway (NB we can also insert a factor of
\hbar/2
inside by multiplying by
2/\hbar
on the outside since only the linear order term is non-zero)

\partial_{t}f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})=\frac{p^{2}}{2ma^{2}}\frac{2}{\hbar}\sin\left(-\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{p}\vec{\partial}_{x}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p})+mV(\bm{x})\cdot\frac{2}{\hbar}\sin\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}\vec{\partial}_{p}\right)f_{W}(\bm{x},\bm{p}).

(D.51)

Even though sine is not a linear function, we can combine these two terms, because the terms
(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{x}\vec{\partial}_{p}-\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\partial\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\partial\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{p}\vec{\partial}_{x})^{n}
for
n\geq 2
